\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}

\displaystyle\mkern-18.0mu\mkern-18.0mu\mkern-18.0mu\mkern-18.0mu\mkern-18.0mu\mkern-18.0mu\mkern-18.0mu\coloneq e^{-q{\cdot}a_{1}}\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{C}(+,+)+e^{+q{\cdot}a_{1}}\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{C}(-,-)

(4.19)

where
\mathcal{C}(\pm,\pm)
denotes the cuts according to the helicity of graviton emitted from the massive
p_{1}
line.

Note that, unlike in the triple cut of Figure
1
, the two three-point amplitudes on the bottom line are indistinguishable since they are symmetric under swapping
\ell_{1}
and
\ell_{2}
, thus we still need the factor of
1/2
for summing over bosons.

We can rewrite this cut combination in terms of
\cosh(a_{1}{\cdot}q)
and
\sinh(a_{1}{\cdot}q)
as follows,

\cosh(a_{1}{\cdot}q)\frac{1}{2}\left(\mathcal{C}(+,+)+\mathcal{C}(-,-)\right)-\sinh(a_{1}{\cdot}q)\frac{1}{2}\left(\mathcal{C}(+,+)-\mathcal{C}(-,-)\right)\,.

(4.20)

To compute the cut contributions, we will rewrite the above expression in terms of symmetric, and anti-symmetric graviton state sums. To do this, we first note that
\mathcal{C}(-,+)=\mathcal{C}(+,-)=0
due to the vanishing of the
R^{3}
Compton amplitude for these helicity configurations. This allows us to rewrite the
\cosh(a_{1}{\cdot}q)
terms as

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\left(\mathcal{C}(+,+)+\mathcal{C}(-,-)\right)=\frac{1}{2}\left(\mathcal{C}(+,+)+\mathcal{C}(+,-)+\mathcal{C}(-,+)+\mathcal{C}(-,-)\right)

(4.21)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{h_{1},h_{2}}\int\frac{d^{D}\ell_{1}}{(2\pi)^{D}}\delta(\ell_{1}^{2})\delta(\ell_{2}^{2})\mathcal{A}_{3}^{h_{1}}(\bar{p}_{1},\ell_{1})\pi\delta(\bar{p}_{1}{\cdot}\ell_{1})\mathcal{A}_{3}^{h_{2}}(\bar{p}_{1},\ell_{2})M_{4,R^{3}}^{h_{1},h_{2}}(\bar{p}_{2},a_{2},\ell_{1},\ell_{2})\,,

and similarly for the
\sinh(a_{1}{\cdot}q)
terms

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\left(\mathcal{C}(+,+)-\mathcal{C}(-,-)\right)=\frac{1}{2}\left(\mathcal{C}(+,+)-\mathcal{C}(+,-)+\mathcal{C}(-,+)-\mathcal{C}(-,-)\right)

(4.22)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{h_{1},h_{2}}\frac{h_{2}}{2}\int\frac{d^{D}\ell_{1}}{(2\pi)^{D}}\delta(\ell_{1}^{2})\delta(\ell_{2}^{2})\mathcal{A}_{3}^{h_{1}}(\bar{p}_{1},\ell_{1})\pi\delta(\bar{p}_{1}{\cdot}\ell_{1})\mathcal{A}_{3}^{h_{2}}(\bar{p}_{1},\ell_{2})M_{4,R^{3}}^{h_{1},h_{2}}(\bar{p}_{2},a_{2},\ell_{1},\ell_{2})\,,

where as usual the sums over helicity take the values
h_{i}=\pm 2
. The
\cosh(a_{1}{\cdot}q)
term (
4.21
) contains only “symmetric” graviton state sums in
h_{1}
and
h_{2}
. By symmetric state sum here we mean the standard completeness relation

\varepsilon_{-k}^{\mu+}\varepsilon_{-k}^{\nu+}\varepsilon_{k}^{\alpha-}\varepsilon_{k}^{\beta-}+\varepsilon_{-k}^{\mu-}\varepsilon_{-k}^{\nu-}\varepsilon_{k}^{\alpha+}\varepsilon_{k}^{\beta+}=\frac{1}{2}\Big(P^{\mu\alpha}P^{\nu\beta}+P^{\mu\beta}P^{\nu\alpha}\Big)-\frac{1}{2}P^{\mu\nu}P^{\alpha\beta}\,,

(4.23)

which is (
4.7
) in the specific case of
D=4
. However, the expression for the
\sinh(a_{1}{\cdot}q)
term (
4.22
) contains an antisymmetric state sum in
h_{2}
which evaluates to

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{-k}^{\mu+}\varepsilon_{-k}^{\nu+}\varepsilon_{k}^{\alpha-}\varepsilon_{k}^{\beta-}-\varepsilon_{-k}^{\mu-}\varepsilon_{-k}^{\nu-}\varepsilon_{k}^{\alpha+}\varepsilon_{k}^{\beta+}=\frac{1}{4}\bigg(P^{\alpha\mu}\tilde{P}^{\beta\nu}+P^{\beta\nu}\tilde{P}^{\alpha\mu}+P^{\beta\mu}\tilde{P}^{\alpha\nu}+P^{\alpha\nu}\tilde{P}^{\beta\mu}\bigg)\,,

(4.24)

where

\tilde{P}^{\mu\nu}\coloneqq i\frac{\epsilon^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}k_{\rho}t_{\sigma}}{k{\cdot}t}\,.

(4.25)

Performing these state sums, we find that the term proportional to
\sinh(a_{1}{\cdot}q)
in the sum (
4.20
) vanishes, and thus the cut (
4.19
) is proportional to
\cosh{(a_{1}{\cdot}q)}
. After lifting off the cut condition we have a tensor triangle integral which we reduce using Passarino-Veltman reduction followed by IBP relations. This, once again, leaves us with a result proportional to the scalar triangle

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{4,I_{1}}

\displaystyle=i\left(\frac{\kappa}{2}\right)^{6}\frac{3}{16}\bar{m}_{1}^{3}\bar{m}_{2}^{2}|q|^{3}\cosh(a_{1}{\cdot}q)\bigg[(\bar{\sigma}^{2}{-}1)\cosh(a_{2}{\cdot}q)-i\bar{\sigma}\frac{\sinh(a_{2}{\cdot}q)}{a_{2}{\cdot}q}\epsilon(\bar{u}_{1}\bar{u}_{2}a_{2}q)\bigg]

\displaystyle+(1{\leftrightarrow}2)\,,

and

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathcal{M}_{4,G_{3}}&=-i\left(\frac{\kappa}{2}\right)^{6}\frac{3}{16}\bar{m}_{1}^{3}\bar{m}_{2}^{2}|q|^{3}\cosh(a_{1}{\cdot}q)\cosh(a_{2}{\cdot}q)+(1\leftrightarrow 2)\,.\end{split}

(4.27)

As before, we can remove the bars from the above expressions and by doing so we recover our earlier results (
3.40
) and (
3.50
).

5
Parity-odd cubic deformations

So far we have considered only corrections to general relativity due to parity-even structures that are cubic in the Riemann tensor,
I_{1}
and
I_{2}
(or
G_{3})
.
It is easy to repeat this analysis for parity-odd versions,
\tilde{I}_{1}
and
\tilde{I}_{2}
, which can for example arise from integrating out (chiral) matter coupled to gravity.
These interactions are obtained from their parity-even cousins by simply replacing one of the three Riemann tensors by its dual version

\displaystyle\tilde{R}^{\mu\nu\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle\coloneq\frac{1}{2}\epsilon^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}{R_{\rho\sigma}}^{\alpha\beta}\ .

(5.1)

This will affect the Compton amplitudes that enter the unitarity cuts/leading singularity computations performed earlier.
First, we notice that we only need to understand how on-shell gravitons are affected. When we compute the parity-odd versions of the vertices (
2.3
) and (
2.4
) we produce terms where the Levi-Civita tensor acts on the off-shell graviton but we can always interpret this Levi-Civita tensor to act on one of the on-shell gravitons. For
\tilde{I}_{1}
this is immediate, while for
\tilde{I}_{2}
this requires some algebra.

In essence we only have to replace one of the on-shell gravitons:

\displaystyle R_{i}^{\alpha\beta\mu\nu}=\frac{\kappa}{2}F_{i}^{\alpha\beta}F_{i}^{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle\rightarrow\tilde{R}_{i}^{\alpha\beta\mu\nu}=\frac{\kappa}{2}\tilde{F}_{i}^{\alpha\beta}F_{i}^{\mu\nu}\ ,

(5.2)

where

\displaystyle\tilde{F}^{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\epsilon^{\alpha\beta\mu\nu}F_{\mu\nu}\ .

(5.3)

Since we are in four dimensions, the gravitons can carry two possible helicities
+
or
-
, which correspond to anti-selfdual (ASD) and selfdual (SD) field strengths, respectively.
In the case of positive helicity (ASD), we have

\displaystyle F^{(+)}=-\ast F^{(+)}=-i\tilde{F}^{(+)}\Rightarrow\tilde{F}^{(+)}=iF^{(+)}\ ,

(5.4)

where
\ast
is the Hodge star operation.
In the case of negative helicity (SD), we have

\displaystyle F^{(-)}=\ast F^{(-)}=i\tilde{F}^{(-)}\Rightarrow\tilde{F}^{(-)}=-iF^{(-)}\ .

(5.5)

As in the parity-even case, both on-shell gravitons must have the same helicity otherwise the vertex/Compton amplitude vanishes. Hence the overall effect is that, for the case of positive helicity, we get an overall factor of
+i
; while for negative helicity we get an overall factor of
-i
for the Compton amplitude. Note that it does not matter which on-shell graviton is replaced by its dual version since both must have the same helicity.

If we now sum in the cut/leading singularity over the two allowed helicity configurations
we get the overall factor

\displaystyle(+i)e^{a_{1}{\cdot}q}+(-i)e^{-a_{1}{\cdot}q}=2i\sinh(a_{1}{\cdot}q)\ ,

(5.6)

instead of
2\cosh(a_{1}{\cdot}q)
in the case of parity-even interactions.

Summarising, in order to get the corrections from the parity-odd, cubic curvature terms we simply have to replace in (
3.50
) the factor
\cosh(a_{1}{\cdot}q)
by

i\sinh(a_{1}{\cdot}q)
. Doing so, we arrive at the results:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathcal{M}_{\tilde{I}_{1}}^{\bigtriangleup}&=-\left(\frac{\kappa}{2}\right)^{6}\frac{3}{16}m_{1}^{3}m_{2}^{2}|q|^{3}\sinh(a_{1}{\cdot}q)\left[(\sigma^{2}-1)\cosh(a_{2}{\cdot}q)-i\sigma\frac{\sinh(a_{2}{\cdot}q)}{a_{2}{\cdot}q}\epsilon(u_{1}u_{2}a_{2}q)\right],\\
\mathcal{M}_{\tilde{G}_{3}}^{\bigtriangleup}&=\left(\frac{\kappa}{2}\right)^{6}\frac{3}{16}m_{1}^{3}m_{2}^{2}|q|^{3}\,\sinh(a_{1}{\cdot}q)\cosh(a_{2}{\cdot}q)\,.\end{split}

(5.7)

As before, the complete amplitudes can be obtained using (
3.38
) and (
3.41
) (see also (
3.37
)). Note however the additional sign that appears compared to the
parity-even case,

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathcal{M}_{\tilde{I}_{1}}^{\bigtriangledown}&=\left(\frac{\kappa}{2}\right)^{6}\frac{3}{16}m_{1}^{2}m_{2}^{3}|q|^{3}\sinh(a_{2}{\cdot}q)\left[(\sigma^{2}-1)\cosh(a_{1}{\cdot}q)-i\sigma\frac{\sinh(a_{1}{\cdot}q)}{a_{1}{\cdot}q}\epsilon(u_{1}u_{2}a_{1}q)\right],\\
\mathcal{M}_{\tilde{G}_{3}}^{\bigtriangledown}&=-\left(\frac{\kappa}{2}\right)^{6}\frac{3}{16}m_{1}^{2}m_{2}^{3}|q|^{3}\,\sinh(a_{2}{\cdot}q)\cosh(a_{1}{\cdot}q)\,.\end{split}

(5.8)

6
Observables from KMOC

We begin with a lightning review of the KMOC approach to classical observables, following
[
133
,
36
]

(see also

[
37
,
71
,
73
]
for some recent applications in the presence of spin).
For any observable
\mathbb{O}
, we want to compute the change

\displaystyle\langle\Delta\mathbb{O}\rangle_{\psi}=\mbox{}_{\rm out}\langle\psi|\mathbb{O}|\psi\rangle_{\rm out}-\mbox{}_{\rm in}\langle\psi|\mathbb{O}|\psi\rangle_{\rm in}=\mbox{}_{\rm in}\langle\psi|S^{\dagger}\mathbb{O}S|\psi\rangle_{\rm in}-\mbox{}_{\rm in}\langle\psi|\mathbb{O}|\psi\rangle_{\rm in}\ .

(6.1)

Using
S=\mathbbm{1}+iT
and unitarity, one arrives at
[
133
]

\displaystyle\langle\Delta\mathbb{O}\rangle_{\psi}=i\ \mbox{}_{\rm in}\langle\psi|[\mathbb{O},T]|\psi\rangle_{\rm in}+\mbox{}_{\rm in}\langle\psi|T^{\dagger}[\mathbb{O},T]|\psi\rangle_{\rm in}\ .

(6.2)

We represent the initial state of the two heavy objects as

\displaystyle|\psi\rangle_{\rm in}\coloneqq\int\!d\Phi(p_{1})d\Phi(p_{2})e^{i(p_{1}{\cdot}b_{1}+p_{2}{\cdot}b_{2})}\phi(p_{1})\phi(p_{2})\xi_{1,i_{1}}\xi_{2,i_{2}}|p_{1},i_{1},p_{2},i_{2}\rangle_{\rm in}\ ,

(6.3)

with the wavefunctions
\phi(p_{1})
and
\phi(p_{2})
being peaked around the classical values of the momenta. We also define

\displaystyle\begin{split}d\Phi(p)&\coloneqq\frac{d^{D}p}{(2\pi)^{D{-}1}}\delta^{(+)}(p^{2}-m^{2})\,,\qquad|p,i\rangle\coloneqq a^{\dagger}_{i}(\vec{p})|0\rangle\,,\end{split}

(6.4)

with
[a_{i}(\vec{p}),a^{\dagger}_{j}(\vec{p}^{\,\prime})]=(2\pi)^{D{-}1}(2E_{p})\delta_{ij}\delta^{(D{-}1)}(\vec{p}-\vec{p}^{\,\prime})
, with the normalisations

\displaystyle\int\!d\Phi(p)\,|\phi(p)|^{2}=\sum_{i}|\xi_{i}|^{2}=1\,.

(6.5)

The state
|p_{1},i_{1},p_{2},i_{2}\rangle_{\rm in}
defines two incoming particles with momenta
p_{1}
and
p_{2}
, and spins
s_{1}
and
s_{2}
respectively. The indices
i_{1}
,
i_{2}
encode the spin degrees of freedom of the two particles, namely they are the little group indices in the spin-
s_{1}
and spin-
s_{2}
representation, such that
i_{k}=1,\dots,2s_{k}+1
. The little group vectors
\xi_{1,i_{1}}
and
\xi_{2,i_{2}}
define a choice of polarisation for the two particles. They are related to the
SU(2)
polarisation variables
z^{(p)}_{a}
introduced in (
3.9
) by a change of basis: namely, the symmetrised tensor product of
2s
spinors, parameterised by the spinor polarisation variables
z^{(p)}_{a}
with
a=1,2
, yields the
2s+1
components encoded in the variables
\xi_{i}
.

Any expectation value of an operator
\mathbb{O}
can then be written as

\displaystyle\begin{split}\langle\mathbb{O}\rangle_{\psi}&=\int\!d\Phi(p_{1})d\Phi(p_{2})\phi(p_{1})\phi(p_{2})\int\!\frac{d^{D}q_{1}}{(2\pi)^{D{-}1}}\frac{d^{D}q_{2}}{(2\pi)^{D{-}1}}\delta(2\bar{p}_{1}{\cdot}q_{1})\delta(2\bar{p}_{2}{\cdot}q_{2})e^{i(q_{1}{\cdot}b_{1}+q_{2}{\cdot}b_{2})}\\
&\phi^{\ast}(p_{1}{-}q_{1})\phi^{\ast}(p_{2}{-}q_{2})\ \bar{\xi}_{1,i^{\prime}_{1}}\bar{\xi}_{2,i^{\prime}_{2}}\langle p_{1}^{\prime},i_{1}^{\prime},p_{2}^{\prime},i_{2}^{\prime}|\mathbb{O}|p_{1},i_{1},p_{2},i_{2}\rangle\xi_{1,i_{1}}\xi_{2,i_{2}}\\
&\!\!\to\int\!d\Phi(p_{1})d\Phi(p_{2})|\phi(p_{1})|^{2}|\phi(p_{2})|^{2}\int\!\frac{d^{D}q_{1}}{(2\pi)^{D{-}1}}\frac{d^{D}q_{2}}{(2\pi)^{D{-}1}}\delta(2\bar{p}_{1}{\cdot}q_{1})\delta(2\bar{p}_{2}{\cdot}q_{2})e^{i(q_{1}{\cdot}b_{1}+q_{2}{\cdot}b_{2})}\\
&\bar{\xi}_{1,i^{\prime}_{1}}\bar{\xi}_{2,i^{\prime}_{2}}\langle p_{1}^{\prime},i_{1}^{\prime},p_{2}^{\prime},i_{2}^{\prime}|\mathbb{O}|p_{1},i_{1},p_{2},i_{2}\rangle\xi_{1,i_{1}}\xi_{2,i_{2}}\,,\end{split}

(6.6)

where,
as usual, in the last step
we have approximated
\phi(p_{i}{-}q_{i}){\to}\phi(p_{i})
, with
i=1,2
.
The barred variables are defined as

\displaystyle\begin{split}p_{1}&=\bar{p}_{1}+\frac{q_{1}}{2}\,,\qquad p_{1}^{\prime}=\bar{p}_{1}-\frac{q_{1}}{2}\,,\\
p_{2}&=\bar{p}_{2}+\frac{q_{2}}{2}\,,\qquad p_{2}^{\prime}=\bar{p}_{2}-\frac{q_{2}}{2}\,,\end{split}

(6.7)

and satisfy

\displaystyle\bar{p}_{1}{\cdot}q_{1}=\bar{p}_{2}{\cdot}q_{2}=0\,.

(6.8)

In the elastic case, that we are studying, we can set

q_{2}=-q_{1}\coloneq q
.

Note that computing classical observables requires a change of variables encoding the spin degrees of freedom
[
36
]
. Namely, for each incoming state of the form

|\psi\rangle=\int\!d\Phi(p)e^{ip{\cdot}b}\phi(p)\xi_{i}|p,i\rangle,

(6.9)

we consider the mass-rescaled Pauli-Lubanski vector
\mathbb{S}^{\mu}
,

\mathbb{S}^{\mu}=\frac{1}{2m}\epsilon^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}\,\mathbb{P}_{\nu}\,\mathbb{M}_{\rho\sigma},

(6.10)

where
\mathbb{M}_{\rho\sigma}
are Lorentz generators, and compute its expectation value,

\langle\psi|\mathbb{S}^{\mu}|\psi\rangle=\int\!d\Phi(p)|\phi(p)|^{2}\,\bar{\xi}_{i}S^{\mu}_{ij}(p)\xi_{j},

(6.11)

where we used
\langle p^{\prime},j|\mathbb{S}^{\mu}|p,i\rangle=(2\pi)^{D{-}1}\delta^{(D{-}1)}(\vec{p}-\vec{p}^{\,\prime})\,(2E_{p})\,S^{\mu}_{ij}(p)
.
The vector

\displaystyle S^{\mu}(p)\coloneqq\bar{\xi}_{i}S^{\mu}_{ij}(p)\xi_{j}

(6.12)

is the angular momentum vector of the classical particle,
and indeed it corresponds to the spin vector of a Kerr black hole, as defined in (
2.11
).
It is also convenient to use the ring radius vector
a^{\mu}(p)\coloneqq S^{\mu}(p)/m
already introduced in Section
2
.

The amplitude
\xi_{1,i_{1}}\xi_{2,i_{2}}\bar{\xi}_{1,i^{\prime}_{1}}\bar{\xi}_{2,i^{\prime}_{2}}\langle p_{1}^{\prime},i_{1}^{\prime},p_{2}^{\prime},i_{2}^{\prime}|\mathbb{O}|p_{1},i_{1},p_{2},i_{2}\rangle
can be written in terms of massive polarisation tensors

\displaystyle\bm{\varepsilon}_{p}^{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{s}}\coloneq\xi_{p,i}\,\varepsilon_{p,i}^{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{s}}\,,

(6.13)

where the bold notation denotes the contraction with the little group variables
\xi_{p,i}
. The spin vector
S^{\mu}(p)
in (
6.12
) can be written in terms of such polarisation tensors,

\displaystyle\begin{split}S^{\mu}(p)=&\langle\psi|\mathbb{S}^{\mu}|\psi\rangle=(\bar{\bm{\varepsilon}}_{p})_{\alpha(s)}{(\mathbb{S}^{\mu})^{\alpha(s)}}_{\beta(s)}(\bm{\varepsilon}_{p})^{\beta(s)}\\
=&\frac{1}{2m}\epsilon^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}p_{\nu}(\bar{\bm{\varepsilon}}_{p})_{\alpha(s)}{(\mathbb{M}_{\rho\sigma})^{\alpha(s)}}_{\beta(s)}(\bm{\varepsilon}_{p})^{\beta(s)},\end{split}

(6.14)

where
\alpha(s)\coloneq\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{s}
and
{(\mathbb{M}_{\rho\sigma})^{\alpha(s)}}_{\beta(s)}=i\,2s\,{\delta^{(\alpha_{1}}}_{[\rho}\eta_{\sigma](\beta_{1}}\delta^{\alpha_{2}}_{\beta_{2}}\dots\delta^{\alpha_{s})}_{\beta_{s})}
, and we have omitted the integration
\int\!d\Phi(p)|\phi(p)|^{2}
, as is customary when using sharply peaked wavepackets
\phi(p)
.
For convenience, we write the polarisation tensor as a product of vectors,

\displaystyle\bm{\varepsilon}_{p}^{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{s}}\to\bm{\varepsilon}_{p}^{\mu_{1}}\dots\bm{\varepsilon}_{p}^{\mu_{s}}\,,

(6.15)

and hence, for a spin-
s
particle we have
17
17

17

See e.g.
[
58
]
.

S^{\mu}(p)=\frac{is}{m}\,(\bar{\bm{\varepsilon}}_{p}{\cdot}\bm{\varepsilon}_{p})^{s-1}\epsilon^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}p_{\nu}\bar{\bm{\varepsilon}}_{p\rho}\bm{\varepsilon}_{p\sigma}.

(6.16)

In order to recast the amplitude in terms of such variables, one needs to write the polarisation vectors
\bm{\varepsilon}_{p_{i}^{\prime}}^{\mu}
as a boost acting of
\bm{\varepsilon}_{p_{i}}^{\mu}
, for instance
[
70
]

\bm{\varepsilon}_{p_{1}^{\prime}}^{\mu}=-\bar{\bm{\varepsilon}}_{p_{1}}^{\mu}-\frac{\bar{\bm{\varepsilon}}_{p_{1}}{\cdot}q}{2m_{1}^{2}\sqrt{1-\frac{q^{2}}{4m_{1}^{2}}}}\Big(p_{1}^{\mu}+\frac{q^{\mu}}{2}\Big),

(6.17)

where
q^{\mu}=p_{1}^{\prime}-p_{1}
.
This requires the identification

\displaystyle\xi_{1^{\prime},i}=\bar{\xi}_{1,i}\,,

(6.18)

effectively an alignment of the polarisation of the incoming and outgoing quantum states describing a single classical object. Interestingly, this is automatic in the KMOC framework, as can be seen in (
6.6
).
An analogous relation can be derived to express
\bm{\varepsilon}_{p_{2^{\prime}}}^{\mu}
in terms of
\bm{\varepsilon}_{p_{2}}^{\mu}
. Once the amplitude is given in terms of
\bm{\varepsilon}_{p_{1}}^{\mu}
and
\bm{\varepsilon}_{p_{2}}^{\mu}
only, relations such as (
6.16
) can be employed to rewrite it as a function of the spin vectors
S_{1}^{\mu}
and
S_{2}^{\mu}
.

One more step required to recover classical physics is the limit
s_{i}{\to}\infty

[
34
,
35
]
. The angular momentum
J
for a spin-
s
quantum particle is of order
J{\sim}\hbar\,s
, therefore if we take the
\hbar\to 0
limit but wish to keep
J
finite, we need
s{\sim}1/\hbar\to\infty
. From (
6.16
), we can see that this implies

S^{\mu}(p)\sim\frac{1}{\hbar},

(6.19)

which we have used in the previous sections.

6.1
The momentum kick

As a first example, we consider the momentum operator
\mathbb{P}_{1}
of particle
1
. From (
6.2
) we have
[
133
]

\displaystyle\langle\Delta\mathbb{P}_{1}^{\mu}\rangle_{\psi}=\langle\Delta\mathbb{P}_{1}^{\mu}\rangle_{\psi}^{(1)}+\langle\Delta\mathbb{P}_{1}^{\mu}\rangle_{\psi}^{(2)}\ ,

(6.20)

with

\displaystyle\begin{split}\langle\Delta\mathbb{P}_{1}^{\mu}\rangle_{\psi}^{(1)}=-\int\!\frac{d^{D}q_{1}}{(2\pi)^{D{-}1}}&\frac{d^{D}q_{2}}{(2\pi)^{D{-}1}}\delta(2\bar{p}_{1}{\cdot}q_{1})\delta(2\bar{p}_{2}{\cdot}q_{2})e^{i(q_{1}{\cdot}b_{1}+q_{2}{\cdot}b_{2})}\,q_{1}^{\mu}\,\\
&\times\bar{\xi}_{1,i^{\prime}_{1}}\bar{\xi}_{2,i^{\prime}_{2}}\langle p_{1}^{\prime},i_{1}^{\prime},p_{2}^{\prime},i_{2}^{\prime}|iT|p_{1},i_{1},p_{2},i_{2}\rangle\xi_{1,i_{1}}\xi_{2,i_{2}}\ ,\end{split}

(6.21)

and

\displaystyle\langle\Delta\mathbb{P}_{1}^{\mu}\rangle_{\psi}^{(2)}=\sum_{X}\int\!d\Phi(r_{1})d\Phi(r_{2})d\Phi(X)\int\!\frac{d^{D}q_{1}}{(2\pi)^{D{-}1}}\frac{d^{D}q_{2}}{(2\pi)^{D{-}1}}\delta(2\bar{p}_{1}{\cdot}q_{1})\delta(2\bar{p}_{2}{\cdot}q_{2})e^{i(q_{1}{\cdot}b_{1}+q_{2}{\cdot}b_{2})}\,

\displaystyle\times(r_{1}{-}p_{1})^{\mu}\bar{\xi}_{1,i^{\prime}_{1}}\bar{\xi}_{2,i^{\prime}_{2}}\,\langle p_{1}^{\prime},i_{1}^{\prime},p_{2}^{\prime},i_{2}^{\prime}|T^{\dagger}|r_{1},j_{1},r_{2},j_{2},X\rangle\langle r_{1},j_{1},r_{2},j_{2},X|T|p_{1},i_{1},p_{2},i_{2}\rangle\xi_{1,i_{1}}\xi_{2,i_{2}}\ ,

or equivalently
18
18

18

Note that in our conventions
\mathcal{M}
denotes a matrix element of
iT
.

\displaystyle\begin{split}\langle\Delta\mathbb{P}_{1}^{\mu}\rangle_{\psi}^{(1)}=\int\!d\mu^{(D)}\,e^{iq{\cdot}b}\,q^{\mu}\,\bar{\xi}_{1,i^{\prime}_{1}}\bar{\xi}_{2,i^{\prime}_{2}}\mathcal{M}_{i^{\prime}_{1}i^{\prime}_{2}i_{1}i_{2}}(p_{1},p_{2}\to p_{1}^{\prime},p_{2}^{\prime})\xi_{1,i_{1}}\xi_{2,i_{2}}\ ,\end{split}

(6.22)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\langle\Delta\mathbb{P}_{1}^{\mu}\rangle_{\psi}^{(2)}=\sum_{X}\int\!d\Phi(r_{1})d\Phi(r_{2})d\Phi(X)\int\!d\mu^{(D)}\,e^{iq{\cdot}b}\,(2\pi)^{D}\delta^{(D)}(p_{1}+p_{2}-r_{1}-r_{2}-r_{X})\,\\
&\qquad(r_{1}{-}p_{1})^{\mu}\,\bar{\xi}_{1,i^{\prime}_{1}}\bar{\xi}_{2,i^{\prime}_{2}}\mathcal{M}^{\ast}_{i^{\prime}_{1}i^{\prime}_{2}j_{1}j_{2}}(p_{1}^{\prime},p_{2}^{\prime}{\to}r_{1},r_{2},X)\mathcal{M}_{j_{1}j_{2}i_{1}i_{2}}(p_{1},p_{2}{\to}r_{1},r_{2},X)\xi_{1,i_{1}}\xi_{2,i_{2}}\ .\end{split}

(6.23)

In these relations
we
have omitted the ubiquitous
\int\!d\Phi(p_{1})d\Phi(p_{2})|\phi(p_{1})|^{2}|\phi(p_{2})|^{2}
integration, and
have introduced the measure

\displaystyle\begin{split}d\mu^{(D)}\coloneqq\frac{\!\!\!\!d^{D}q}{(2\pi)^{D-2}}\,\delta(2{\bar{p}}_{1}{\cdot}q)\delta(2{\bar{p}}_{2}{\cdot}q)\,,\end{split}

(6.24)

where
b\coloneq b_{2}{-}b_{1}
, and we recall that
q=p_{1}^{\prime}-p_{1}
.

Finally, we note that the second KMOC term
\langle\Delta\mathbb{P}_{1}^{\mu}\rangle_{\psi}^{(2)}
in (
6.23
)
contains the product of two amplitudes, which will necessarily be tree amplitudes at our leading order.
Since there are no tree-level contributions with an insertion of an
R^{3}
vertex to two-to-two scattering, we conclude that at leading order, the momentum kick in the presence of cubic deformations is fully captured by the term

\langle\Delta\mathbb{P}_{1}^{\mu}\rangle_{\psi}^{(1)}
in (
6.22
).

6.2
The spin kick

Similarly to the momentum kick, we can obtain the spin kick from KMOC, following
[
36
]
. The observable of interest here is
\mathbb{O}=\mathbb{S}_{1}^{\mu}
, the mass-rescaled Pauli-Lubanski vector from (
6.10
) of particle
1
.

Starting from (
6.2
) and focusing on the first term, we get

\displaystyle\langle\Delta\mathbb{S}_{1}^{\mu}\rangle_{\psi}^{(1)}

\displaystyle=i\int\!\frac{d^{D}q_{1}}{(2\pi)^{D{-}1}}\frac{d^{D}q_{2}}{(2\pi)^{D{-}1}}\delta(2\bar{p}_{1}{\cdot}q_{1})\delta(2\bar{p}_{2}{\cdot}q_{2})e^{i(q_{1}{\cdot}b_{1}+q_{2}{\cdot}b_{2})}

\displaystyle\times\bar{\xi}_{1,i^{\prime}_{1}}\bar{\xi}_{2,i^{\prime}_{2}}\ \langle p_{1}^{\prime},i_{1}^{\prime},p_{2}^{\prime},i_{2}^{\prime}|\mathbb{S}^{\mu}_{1}T-T\mathbb{S}^{\mu}_{1}|p_{1},i_{1},p_{2},i_{2}\rangle\xi_{1,i_{1}}\xi_{2,i_{2}}\,.

(6.25)

Inserting a complete set of states,

\mathbbm{1}=\sum_{j_{1},j_{2}}\int\!d\Phi(r_{1})d\Phi(r_{2})|r_{1},j_{1},r_{2},j_{2}\rangle\langle r_{1},j_{1},r_{2},j_{2}|\,,

(6.26)

we can write (
6.25
) in terms of the matrix elements of
\mathbb{S}_{1}^{\mu}
and
iT
, so that

\displaystyle\langle\Delta\mathbb{S}_{1}^{\mu}\rangle_{\psi}^{(1)}=\int\!

\displaystyle\frac{d^{D}q_{1}}{(2\pi)^{D{-}1}}\frac{d^{D}q_{2}}{(2\pi)^{D{-}1}}\delta(2\bar{p}_{1}{\cdot}q_{1})\delta(2\bar{p}_{2}{\cdot}q_{2})e^{i(q_{1}{\cdot}b_{1}+q_{2}{\cdot}b_{2})}(2\pi)^{D}\delta^{(D)}(q_{1}+q_{2})

\displaystyle\bar{\xi}_{1,i^{\prime}_{1}}\bar{\xi}_{2,i^{\prime}_{2}}\left(S^{\mu}_{1\,i_{1}^{\prime}j_{1}}(p^{\prime}_{1})\mathcal{M}_{j_{1}i_{2}^{\prime}i_{1}i_{2}}-\mathcal{M}_{i_{1}^{\prime}i_{2}^{\prime}j_{1}i_{2}}S^{\mu}_{1\,j_{1}i_{1}}(p_{1})\right)\xi_{1,i_{1}}\xi_{2,i_{2}}\,,

(6.27)

where
\mathcal{M}=\mathcal{M}(p_{1},p_{2}\to p^{\prime}_{1},p^{\prime}_{2})
. The delta function enforces
q_{2}=-q_{1}\coloneq q
, and thus
p^{\prime}_{1}=p_{1}+q
, which is related by an infinitesimal Lorentz transformation to
p_{1}
, meaning that the spin

S^{\mu}_{1\,ij}(p^{\prime}_{1})=S^{\mu}_{1\,ij}(p_{1}+q)=S^{\mu}_{1\,ij}(p_{1})-\frac{q{\cdot}S_{1\,ij}(p_{1})}{m_{1}^{2}}p^{\mu}_{1}\,+\mathcal{O}(\hbar^{2})\,.

(6.28)

Similarly to the case of the momentum kick,
at leading order only
\langle\Delta\mathbb{S}_{1}^{\mu}\rangle_{\psi}^{(1)}
contributes, since
\langle\Delta\mathbb{S}_{1}^{\mu}\rangle_{\psi}^{(2)}
contains
the product of two tree-level two-to-two amplitudes, and there is no tree-level contribution to this process from a cubic vertex.

Summarising, the leading contribution to the spin kick becomes

\langle\Delta\mathbb{S}_{1}^{\mu}\rangle_{\psi}^{(1)}=\int\!d\mu^{(D)}e^{iq{\cdot}b}\left(\left[S_{1}^{\mu}(p_{1}),\mathcal{M}\right]-\frac{q{\cdot}S_{1}(p_{1})}{m_{1}^{2}}p^{\mu}_{1}\,\mathcal{M}\right)\,.

(6.29)

Here, the little group indices should be understood to be arranged as in (
6.27
). This cleanly decomposes the spin kick into a longitudinal and transverse part (by virtue of
p_{1}{\cdot}S_{1}(p_{1})=0
). We can recast this in terms of derivatives of the Fourier-transformed amplitude as was done in
[
37
]
to give

\langle\Delta\mathbb{S}_{1}^{\mu}\rangle_{\psi}^{(1)}=\frac{i}{m_{1}}\left(p^{\mu}_{1}a^{\nu}_{1}\frac{\partial}{\partial b^{\nu}}+\epsilon^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}p_{1\nu}a_{1\rho}\frac{\partial}{\partial a_{1}^{\sigma}}\right)\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}(b)\,,

(6.30)

where

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}(b)\coloneq\int\!\frac{d^{D}q}{(2\pi)^{D-2}}\delta(2{\bar{p}}_{1}{\cdot}q)\delta(2{\bar{p}}_{2}{\cdot}q)e^{iq{\cdot}b}\mathcal{M}\,,

(6.31)

is the amplitude in impact parameter space.
This takes advantage of the fact that under the integral we can make the replacement
iq_{\mu}\mapsto\partial/\partial b^{\mu}
, and that

\left[A,f(B)\right]=\left[A,B\right]\frac{\partial f}{\partial B}
, with
A
and
B
being
S_{1}^{\mu}
,
S_{1}^{\nu}
, respectively and
f(B)
the amplitude
\mathcal{M}
.
Finally, we also comment that, because of the absence of a hyperclassical contribution to
\mathcal{M}
, we can safely remove the bars in
\bar{p}_{1,2}
.

6.3
Fourier transforms to impact parameter space

First we recall that

\displaystyle\int\!\frac{d^{D}q}{(2\pi)^{D-2}}\delta(2{p}_{1}{\cdot}q)\delta(2{p}_{2}{\cdot}q)e^{iq{\cdot}b}(\cdots)=\frac{1}{4m_{1}m_{2}\sqrt{\sigma^{2}-1}}\int\!\frac{d^{D-2}q}{(2\pi)^{D-2}}e^{-i\vec{q}{\cdot}\vec{b}}(\cdots)\ ,

(6.32)

where
\sigma=p_{1}{\cdot}p_{2}/(m_{1}m_{2})
.
Note that the momentum transfer
q^{\mu}
is orthogonal to the scattering plane, defined by the incoming momenta
p_{1}
and
p_{2}
, hence the Fourier transform is a two-dimensional integral.
It is convenient to work in the centre of mass frame of the two bodies, where
q^{\mu}=(0,\vec{q})
. In order to extract classical observables, as discussed in the previous sections, we need to compute the Fourier transform
\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}(b)
of the classical amplitudes given in (
4.3
), (
4.27
) and (
5.7
). This requires computing the following Fourier transforms,

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{1}\coloneq

\displaystyle\int\!\frac{d^{D-2}q}{(2\pi)^{D-2}}e^{-i\vec{q}{\cdot}\vec{b}}\,|\vec{q}\,|^{3}\cosh{\vec{a}_{1}{\cdot}\vec{q}}\ \cosh{\vec{a}_{2}{\cdot}\vec{q}},

(6.33)

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathcal{F}}_{1}\coloneq

\displaystyle\int\!\frac{d^{D-2}q}{(2\pi)^{D-2}}e^{-i\vec{q}{\cdot}\vec{b}}\,|\vec{q}\,|^{3}\sinh{\vec{a}_{1}{\cdot}\vec{q}}\ \cosh{\vec{a}_{2}{\cdot}\vec{q}},

(6.34)

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{2}\coloneq

\displaystyle\int\!\frac{d^{D-2}q}{(2\pi)^{D-2}}e^{-i\vec{q}{\cdot}\vec{b}}\,|\vec{q}\,|^{3}(\vec{\epsilon}_{2}{\cdot}\vec{q})\cosh{\vec{a}_{1}{\cdot}\vec{q}}\ \frac{\sinh{\vec{a}_{2}{\cdot}\vec{q}}}{\vec{a}_{2}{\cdot}\vec{q}},

(6.35)

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathcal{F}}_{2}\coloneq

\displaystyle\int\!\frac{d^{D-2}q}{(2\pi)^{D-2}}e^{-i\vec{q}{\cdot}\vec{b}}\,|\vec{q}\,|^{3}(\vec{\epsilon}_{2}{\cdot}\vec{q})\sinh{\vec{a}_{1}{\cdot}\vec{q}}\ \frac{\sinh{\vec{a}_{2}{\cdot}\vec{q}}}{\vec{a}_{2}{\cdot}\vec{q}}\,,

(6.36)

where
\vec{\epsilon}_{2}{\cdot}\vec{q}\coloneq\epsilon(u_{1},u_{2},a_{2},q)
.
In order to do so, we can rewrite the hyperbolic functions as operators in impact parameter space,

\displaystyle\begin{split}\cosh{\vec{a}_{i}{\cdot}\vec{q}}=\frac{1}{2}(e^{\vec{a}_{i}{\cdot}\vec{q}}+e^{-\vec{a}_{i}{\cdot}\vec{q}})\to\frac{1}{2}(e^{i\vec{a}_{i}{\cdot}\vec{\nabla}_{b}}+e^{-i\vec{a}_{i}{\cdot}\vec{\nabla}_{b}}),\\
\sinh{\vec{a}_{i}{\cdot}\vec{q}}=\frac{1}{2}(e^{\vec{a}_{i}{\cdot}\vec{q}}-e^{-\vec{a}_{i}{\cdot}\vec{q}})\to\frac{1}{2}(e^{i\vec{a}_{i}{\cdot}\vec{\nabla}_{b}}-e^{-i\vec{a}_{i}{\cdot}\vec{\nabla}_{b}}).\end{split}

(6.37)

This reduces the problem to the evaluation of the following integrals,

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\int\!\frac{d^{D-2}q}{(2\pi)^{D-2}}e^{-i\vec{q}{\cdot}\vec{b}}\,|\vec{q}\,|^{3},\\
&\int\!\frac{d^{D-2}q}{(2\pi)^{D-2}}e^{-i\vec{q}{\cdot}\vec{b}}\,|\vec{q}\,|^{3}\frac{1}{\vec{a}_{2}{\cdot}\vec{q}}\,,\end{split}

(6.38)

and to include the additional factor of
\vec{\epsilon}_{2}{\cdot}\vec{q}
from (
6.35
) and (
6.36
) we simply express
\vec{q}
as a derivative in
\vec{b}
.
The first integral in (
6.38
) is a special case of a known result,
19
19

19

See e.g. (A.3) in
[
117
]
.

\int\!\frac{d^{d}q}{(2\pi)^{d}}e^{-i\vec{q}{\cdot}\vec{b}}|\vec{q}\,|^{\alpha}=\left(\frac{2}{|\vec{b}|}\right)^{d+\alpha}\frac{\Gamma\left(\frac{d+\alpha}{2}\right)}{(4\pi)^{d/2}\Gamma\left(-\frac{\alpha}{2}\right)}\xrightarrow[d=2]{\alpha=3}\frac{9}{2\pi|\vec{b}|^{5}}.

(6.39)

To evaluate the second, we use
\vec{a}_{2}=i\vec{\tilde{a}}_{2}
and obtain

\displaystyle\int\!\frac{d^{D-2}q}{(2\pi)^{D-2}}e^{-i\vec{q}{\cdot}\vec{b}}|\vec{q}\,|^{3}\frac{1}{i\vec{\tilde{a}}_{2}{\cdot}\vec{q}+\epsilon}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\!dt\,\int\!\frac{d^{D-2}q}{(2\pi)^{D-2}}e^{-i\vec{q}{\cdot}(\vec{b}+t\vec{\tilde{a}}_{2})-\epsilon t}|\vec{q}\,|^{3}

\displaystyle=\frac{9}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{\infty}dt\,e^{-\epsilon t}\frac{1}{[(\vec{b}+t\vec{\tilde{a}}_{2})^{2}]^{5/2}},

(6.40)

where we have introduced a regulator
\epsilon
, to be taken to zero at a later stage, to ensure convergence of the integrals.

It is now convenient to act on (
6.40
) with the operator
\sinh{i\vec{a}_{2}{\cdot}\vec{\nabla}_{b}}=-\sinh{\vec{\tilde{a}}_{2}{\cdot}\vec{\nabla}_{b}}
, as defined in (
6.37
), to obtain

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathcal{J}&\coloneq\int\!\frac{d^{D-2}q}{(2\pi)^{D-2}}e^{-i\vec{q}{\cdot}\vec{b}}\,|\vec{q}\,|^{3}\frac{\sinh{\vec{a}_{2}{\cdot}\vec{q}}}{\vec{a}_{2}{\cdot}\vec{q}}\\
&=\frac{9}{4\pi}\int_{0}^{\infty}\!dt\,\left(\frac{1}{[(\vec{b}+(t-1)\vec{\tilde{a}}_{2})^{2}]^{5/2}}-\frac{1}{[(\vec{b}+(t+1)\vec{\tilde{a}}_{2})^{2}]^{5/2}}\right)\\
&=\frac{9}{4\pi}\left(\int_{-1}^{\infty}\!dt\,\frac{1}{[(\vec{b}+t\vec{\tilde{a}}_{2})^{2}]^{5/2}}-\int_{1}^{\infty}dt\frac{1}{[(\vec{b}+t\vec{\tilde{a}}_{2})^{2}]^{5/2}}\right)\\
&=\frac{9}{4\pi}\int_{-1}^{+1}\!dt\,\frac{1}{[(\vec{b}+t\vec{\tilde{a}}_{2})^{2}]^{5/2}}\ ,\end{split}

(6.41)

where we have set
\epsilon=0
since the integrals are now convergent and we recall that we have defined
\vec{a}_{2}=i\vec{\tilde{a}}_{2}
.
The last integral can be done explicitly, giving

\mathcal{J}=\frac{9}{4\pi}\int_{-1}^{1}\!dt\,\frac{1}{(-|\vec{a}_{2}|^{2}t^{2}+i2\vec{a}_{2}{\cdot}\vec{b}t+|\vec{b}|^{2})^{5/2}}=\frac{3i\vec{a}_{2}{\cdot}\vec{b}_{+}(N-2\vec{a}_{2}^{\,2}\,\vec{b}_{+}^{\,2})}{4\pi N^{2}(\vec{b}_{+}^{\,2})^{3/2}}+{\rm c.c.}\,,

(6.42)

where
\vec{b}_{\pm}\coloneq\vec{b}\pm i\vec{a_{2}}
and
N\coloneq(\vec{a}_{2}{\cdot}\vec{b})^{2}-\vec{a}_{2}^{\,2}\,\vec{b}^{\,2}=(1/4)\big[\vec{b}_{+}^{\,2}\vec{b}_{-}^{\,2}-(\vec{b}_{+}{\cdot}\vec{b}_{-})^{2}\big]
.

As mentioned earlier, we can now include the factor of
\vec{\epsilon}_{2}{\cdot}\vec{q}
in (
6.35
) and (
6.36
) by writing
\vec{q}
as a derivative in
\vec{b}
, yielding

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathcal{E}&\coloneq\int\!\frac{d^{D-2}q}{(2\pi)^{D-2}}e^{-i\vec{q}{\cdot}\vec{b}}\,|\vec{q}\,|^{3}(\vec{\epsilon}_{2}{\cdot}\vec{q})\ \frac{\sinh{\vec{a}_{2}{\cdot}\vec{q}}}{\vec{a}_{2}{\cdot}\vec{q}}=i\,\vec{\epsilon}_{2}{\cdot}\frac{\partial}{\partial\vec{b}}\,\mathcal{J}\\
&=\frac{3}{4\pi}\frac{\vec{a}_{2}{\cdot}\vec{b}_{+}\,\vec{\epsilon}_{2}{\cdot}\vec{b}}{N(\vec{b}_{+}^{\,2})^{3/2}}\left(\frac{3}{\vec{b}_{+}^{2}}-\frac{4\vec{a}_{2}^{\,2}(N-2\vec{a}_{2}^{\,2}\,\vec{b}_{+}^{\,2})}{N^{2}}\right)-{\rm c.c.}\,.\end{split}

(6.43)

It is easy to obtain
\mathcal{F}_{1}
and
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{1}
by applying the shift-operators (
6.37
) on to (
6.39
),

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{1}

\displaystyle=\frac{9}{8\pi}\sum_{\sigma_{1}=\pm 1}\sum_{\sigma_{2}=\pm 1}\frac{1}{[(\vec{b}+\sigma_{1}i\vec{a}_{1}+\sigma_{2}i\vec{a}_{2})^{2}]^{5/2}}\ ,

(6.44)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{1}

\displaystyle=\frac{9}{8\pi}\sum_{\sigma_{1}=\pm 1}\sum_{\sigma_{2}=\pm 1}\frac{\sigma_{1}}{[(\vec{b}+\sigma_{1}i\vec{a}_{1}+\sigma_{2}i\vec{a}_{2})^{2}]^{5/2}}\ ,

(6.45)

and similarly
\mathcal{F}_{2}
and
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{2}
are expressible in terms of
\mathcal{J}
using the shift operators as

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathcal{F}_{2}&=\frac{1}{2}\left(\mathcal{E}|_{\vec{b}\to\vec{b}+i\vec{a}_{1}}+\mathcal{E}|_{\vec{b}\to\vec{b}-i\vec{a}_{1}}\right)\ ,\\
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{2}&=\frac{1}{2}\left(\mathcal{E}|_{\vec{b}\to\vec{b}+i\vec{a}_{1}}-\mathcal{E}|_{\vec{b}\to\vec{b}-i\vec{a}_{1}}\right)\ .\end{split}\textit{}

(6.46)

6.4
Explicit results and discussion

We provide the general, somewhat lengthy results for the momentum and spin kicks in our
Cubic Corrections to Spinning Observables from Amplitudes
GitHub repository
.
For illustration, we will now present explicit expressions of these results in a number of special cases. To display the results it is useful to return to four-vector notation for the impact parameter and spin vectors. We also define a projector to the plane orthogonal to
u_{1}
and
u_{2}
as follows,

\displaystyle\begin{split}\Pi^{\mu\nu}\coloneq\frac{\epsilon(\mu,\rho,u_{1},u_{2})\epsilon(\nu,\rho,u_{1},u_{2})}{\sigma^{2}-1}&=\eta^{\mu\nu}{-}\frac{(u_{1}^{\mu}{+}u_{2}^{\mu})(u_{1}^{\nu}+u_{2}^{\nu})}{2(\sigma+1)}{+}\frac{(u_{1}^{\mu}-u_{2}^{\mu})(u_{1}^{\nu}-u_{2}^{\nu})}{2(\sigma-1)}\\
&=\eta^{\mu\nu}-u_{1}^{\mu}\check{u}_{1}^{\nu}-u_{2}^{\mu}\check{u}_{2}^{\nu}\,,\end{split}

(6.47)

where the dual four-velocities are defined as
[
21
]

\displaystyle\check{u}_{1}^{\mu}\coloneq\frac{\sigma u_{2}^{\mu}-u_{1}^{\mu}}{\sigma^{2}-1}\,,\qquad\check{u}_{2}^{\mu}\coloneq\frac{\sigma u_{1}^{\mu}-u_{2}^{\mu}}{\sigma^{2}-1}\,,

(6.48)

with

\displaystyle\check{u}_{i}{\cdot}u_{j}=\delta_{ij}\,,\qquad i,j=1,2\,.

(6.49)

Then the projected impact parameter and spin vectors are

b_{\perp}^{\mu}=\Pi^{\mu\nu}b_{\nu}=(0,\vec{b})^{\mu},\qquad a_{i\perp}^{\mu}=\Pi^{\mu\nu}a_{i\nu}=(0,\vec{a}_{i})^{\mu}\,,

(6.50)

which allows us to rewrite the results of the previous section, where the last equalities in both equations above are specific to the centre of mass frame.

6.4.1
Results for the momentum kick

The integrals we need in order to compute the impulse are simply derivatives in
b^{\mu}
of the integrals in (
6.33
):

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}_{1}^{\mu}\coloneq

\displaystyle\int\!\frac{d^{D-2}q_{\perp}}{(2\pi)^{D-2}}e^{iq_{\perp}{\cdot}b_{\perp}}\,q_{\perp}^{\mu}|q_{\perp}\,|^{3}\cosh{a_{1\perp}{\cdot}q_{\perp}}\ \cosh{a_{2\perp}{\cdot}q_{\perp}},

(6.51)

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathcal{B}}_{1}^{\mu}\coloneq

\displaystyle\int\!\frac{d^{D-2}q_{\perp}}{(2\pi)^{D-2}}e^{iq_{\perp}{\cdot}b_{\perp}}\,q_{\perp}^{\mu}|q_{\perp}\,|^{3}\sinh{a_{1\perp}{\cdot}q_{\perp}}\ \cosh{a_{2\perp}{\cdot}q_{\perp}},

(6.52)

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}_{2}^{\mu}\coloneq

\displaystyle\int\!\frac{d^{D-2}q_{\perp}}{(2\pi)^{D-2}}e^{iq_{\perp}{\cdot}b_{\perp}}\,q_{\perp}^{\mu}|q_{\perp}\,|^{3}(\epsilon_{2}{\cdot}q_{\perp})\cosh{a_{1\perp}{\cdot}q_{\perp}}\ \frac{\sinh{a_{2\perp}{\cdot}q_{\perp}}}{a_{2\perp}{\cdot}q_{\perp}},

(6.53)

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathcal{B}}_{2}^{\mu}\coloneq

\displaystyle\int\!\frac{d^{D-2}q_{\perp}}{(2\pi)^{D-2}}e^{iq_{\perp}{\cdot}b_{\perp}}\,q_{\perp}^{\mu}|q_{\perp}\,|^{3}(\epsilon_{2}{\cdot}q_{\perp})\sinh{a_{1\perp}{\cdot}q_{\perp}}\ \frac{\sinh{a_{2\perp}{\cdot}q_{\perp}}}{a_{2\perp}{\cdot}q_{\perp}}\,,

(6.54)

where
\epsilon_{2}{\cdot}q_{\perp}\coloneq\epsilon(u_{1},u_{2},a_{2},q)
. The results for these integrals can be written using the expressions

\displaystyle b_{\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}}^{\mu}\coloneq b_{\perp}^{\mu}+\sigma_{1}ia_{1\perp}^{\mu}+\sigma_{2}ia_{2\perp}^{\mu}\,,\quad N_{\sigma_{1}}\coloneq(a_{2\perp}{\cdot}b_{\sigma_{1}0})^{2}-a_{2\perp}^{2}b_{\sigma_{1}0}^{2}\,,

(6.55)

and

\displaystyle\begin{split}&c_{\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}}^{\mu}\coloneq\frac{1}{N_{\sigma_{1}}^{4}}\Big[\epsilon(\mu a_{2\perp}u_{1}u_{2})N_{\sigma_{1}}b_{{\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}}}^{2}a_{2\perp}{\cdot}b_{{\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}}}\big(3N_{\sigma_{1}}^{2}{-}4a_{2\perp}^{2}b_{{\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}}}^{2}\big(N_{\sigma_{1}}{-}2a_{2\perp}^{2}b_{{\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}}}^{2}\big)\big)\\
&\quad{+}3\epsilon(a_{2\perp}b_{\perp}u_{1}u_{2})\Big(b_{\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}}^{\mu}a_{2\perp}{\cdot}b_{{\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}}}\big(5N_{\sigma_{1}}^{3}{-}2a_{2\perp}^{2}b_{{\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}}}^{2}\big(4a_{2\perp}^{2}b_{{\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}}}^{2}\big(2a_{2\perp}^{2}b_{{\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}}}^{2}{-}N_{\sigma_{1}}\big){+}3N_{\sigma_{1}}^{2}\big)\big)\\
&\quad{+}a_{2\perp}^{\mu}b_{{\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}}}^{2}\big(2a_{2\perp}^{2}b_{{\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}}}^{2}\big(4a_{2\perp}^{2}b_{{\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}}}^{2}\big(2a_{2\perp}^{2}b_{{\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}}}^{2}{+}{N_{\sigma_{1}}}\big){-}N_{\sigma_{1}}^{2}\big){+}N_{\sigma_{1}}^{3}\big)\Big)\Big]\,.\end{split}

(6.56)

Explicitly we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}_{1}^{\mu}=-\frac{45i}{8\pi}\sum_{\sigma_{1}=\pm 1}\sum_{\sigma_{2}=\pm 1}\frac{b_{\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}}^{\mu}}{|b_{\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}}|^{7}}\ ,

(6.57)

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathcal{B}}_{1}^{\mu}=-\frac{45i}{8\pi}\sum_{\sigma_{1}=\pm 1}\sum_{\sigma_{2}=\pm 1}\frac{(-\sigma_{1})\,b_{\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}}^{\mu}}{|b_{\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}}|^{7}}\ ,

(6.58)

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}_{2}^{\mu}=\frac{3i}{8\pi}\sum_{\sigma_{1}=\pm 1}\sum_{\sigma_{2}=\pm 1}\frac{\sigma_{2}c^{\mu}_{\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}}}{|b_{\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}}|^{7}}\,,

(6.59)

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathcal{B}}_{2}^{\mu}=\frac{3i}{8\pi}\sum_{\sigma_{1}=\pm 1}\sum_{\sigma_{2}=\pm 1}\frac{(-\sigma_{1})\sigma_{2}c^{\mu}_{\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}}}{|b_{\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}}|^{7}}\,,

(6.60)

which are the building blocks of the impulse

\displaystyle\langle\Delta\mathbb{P}_{1}^{\mu}\rangle_{I_{1}}

\displaystyle=i\left(\frac{\kappa}{2}\right)^{6}\frac{3}{64}\frac{m_{1}^{2}m_{2}}{\sqrt{\sigma^{2}-1}}\Big((\sigma^{2}-1)\mathcal{B}^{\mu}_{1}-i\sigma\mathcal{B}^{\mu}_{2}\Big)+(1\leftrightarrow 2)\,,

(6.61)

\displaystyle\langle\Delta\mathbb{P}_{1}^{\mu}\rangle_{G_{3}}

\displaystyle=-i\left(\frac{\kappa}{2}\right)^{6}\frac{3}{64}\frac{m_{1}^{2}m_{2}}{\sqrt{\sigma^{2}-1}}\mathcal{B}^{\mu}_{1}+(1\leftrightarrow 2)\,,

(6.62)

\displaystyle\langle\Delta\mathbb{P}_{1}^{\mu}\rangle_{\tilde{I}_{1}}

\displaystyle=-\left(\frac{\kappa}{2}\right)^{6}\frac{3}{64}\frac{m_{1}^{2}m_{2}}{\sqrt{\sigma^{2}-1}}\Big((\sigma^{2}-1)\tilde{\mathcal{B}}^{\mu}_{1}-i\sigma\tilde{\mathcal{B}}^{\mu}_{2}\Big)-(1\leftrightarrow 2)\,,

(6.63)

\displaystyle\langle\Delta\mathbb{P}_{1}^{\mu}\rangle_{\tilde{G}_{3}}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{\kappa}{2}\right)^{6}\frac{3}{64}\frac{m_{1}^{2}m_{2}}{\sqrt{\sigma^{2}-1}}\tilde{\mathcal{B}}^{\mu}_{1}-(1\leftrightarrow 2)\,.

(6.64)

By
(1\leftrightarrow 2)
here we mean swapping
a_{1}\leftrightarrow a_{2}
and
m_{1}\leftrightarrow m_{2}
.
The exact results for the spin kick are rather complicated, hence we will only present expressions that are either expanded to linear order in the spins in Section
6.4.2
, or in the aligned spin case in Section
6.4.3
.

6.4.2
Expanded in spin momentum and spin kicks

The leading-order momentum kick of particle
1
due to the
R^{3}
corrections can be obtained from the general expression (
6.22
). Expanding to linear order in the spins we get, for the parity-even and parity-odd cubic deformations:

\displaystyle\langle\Delta\mathbb{P}_{1}^{\mu}\rangle_{I_{1}}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{\kappa}{2}\right)^{6}\frac{135}{128\pi}\bigg\{\sqrt{\sigma^{2}-1}m_{1}m_{2}(m_{1}+m_{2})\frac{b_{\perp}^{\mu}}{|b_{\perp}|^{7}}

\displaystyle-\frac{\sigma}{\sqrt{\sigma^{2}-1}}\frac{m_{1}m_{2}}{|b_{\perp}|^{9}}\Big[7b_{\perp}^{\mu}\big(m_{1}\epsilon(u_{1}u_{2}a_{2}b_{\perp})+m_{2}\epsilon(u_{1}u_{2}a_{1}b_{\perp})\big)

\displaystyle+|b_{\perp}|^{2}\big(m_{1}\epsilon(u_{1}u_{2}a_{2}\mu)+m_{2}\epsilon(u_{1}u_{2}a_{1}\mu)\big)\Big]\bigg\}\,,

(6.65)

\displaystyle\langle\Delta\mathbb{P}_{1}^{\mu}\rangle_{G_{3}}

\displaystyle=-\left(\frac{\kappa}{2}\right)^{6}\frac{135}{128\pi}\frac{m_{1}m_{2}(m_{1}+m_{2})}{\sqrt{\sigma^{2}-1}}\frac{b_{\perp}^{\mu}}{|b_{\perp}|^{7}}\,,

(6.66)

\displaystyle\langle\Delta\mathbb{P}_{1}^{\mu}\rangle_{\tilde{I}_{1}}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{\kappa}{2}\right)^{6}\frac{135}{128\pi}\sqrt{\sigma^{2}-1}\frac{m_{1}m_{2}}{|b_{\perp}|^{9}}\Big[7b^{\mu}_{\perp}(m_{1}a_{1}{\cdot}b_{\perp}-m_{2}a_{2}{\cdot}b_{\perp})

\displaystyle+|b_{\perp}|^{2}(m_{1}a_{1\perp}^{\mu}-m_{2}a_{2\perp}^{\mu})\Big]

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{\kappa}{2}\right)^{6}\frac{135}{128\pi}\sqrt{\sigma^{2}-1}\frac{m_{1}m_{2}}{|b_{\perp}|^{9}}\big(S_{1\nu}-S_{2\nu}\big)\big(|b_{\perp}|^{2}\Pi^{\mu\nu}+7b_{\perp}^{\mu}b_{\perp}^{\nu}\big)\,,

(6.67)

\displaystyle\langle\Delta\mathbb{P}_{1}^{\mu}\rangle_{\tilde{G}_{3}}

\displaystyle=-\left(\frac{\kappa}{2}\right)^{6}\frac{135}{128\pi}\frac{1}{\sqrt{\sigma^{2}-1}}\frac{m_{1}m_{2}}{|b_{\perp}|^{9}}\Big[7b^{\mu}_{\perp}(m_{1}a_{1}{\cdot}b_{\perp}-m_{2}a_{2}{\cdot}b_{\perp})

\displaystyle+|b_{\perp}|^{2}(m_{1}a_{1\perp}^{\mu}-m_{2}a_{2\perp}^{\mu})\Big]

\displaystyle=-\left(\frac{\kappa}{2}\right)^{6}\frac{135}{128\pi}\frac{1}{\sqrt{\sigma^{2}-1}}\frac{m_{1}m_{2}}{|b_{\perp}|^{9}}\big(S_{1\nu}-S_{2\nu}\big)\big(|b_{\perp}|^{2}\Pi^{\mu\nu}+7b_{\perp}^{\mu}b_{\perp}^{\nu}\big)\,,

(6.68)

where
|b_{\perp}|=\sqrt{-b_{\perp}{\cdot}b_{\perp}}
, and
\Pi^{\mu\nu}
was defined in (
6.47
). Note that the corrections due to spin for
G_{3}
occur only at quadratic order in the
a_{i}
.

Using (
6.30
) we get, for the spin kick of particle
1
expanded to first order in the spins
a_{i}
,

\displaystyle\langle\Delta\mathbb{S}_{1}^{\mu}\rangle_{I_{1}}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{\kappa}{2}\right)^{6}\frac{135}{128\pi}\frac{1}{\sqrt{\sigma^{2}-1}}\frac{m_{1}m_{2}}{|b_{\perp}|^{7}}\Big[m_{2}\sigma\,a_{1}{\cdot}u_{2}b_{\perp}^{\mu}

\displaystyle+a_{1\perp}{\cdot}b_{\perp}\big[-(\sigma^{2}-1)m_{1}u_{1}^{\mu}+m_{2}(u_{1}^{\mu}-\sigma u_{2}^{\mu})\big]\Big]\,,

(6.69)

\displaystyle\langle\Delta\mathbb{S}_{1}^{\mu}\rangle_{G_{3}}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{\kappa}{2}\right)^{6}\frac{135}{128\pi}\frac{1}{\sqrt{\sigma^{2}-1}}\frac{m_{1}m_{2}}{|b_{\perp}|^{7}}(m_{1}+m_{2})(a_{1\perp}{\cdot}b_{\perp})u_{1}^{\mu}\,,

(6.70)

\displaystyle\langle\Delta\mathbb{S}_{1}^{\mu}\rangle_{\tilde{I}_{1}}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{\kappa}{2}\right)^{6}\frac{135}{128\pi}\sqrt{\sigma^{2}-1}\frac{m_{1}^{2}m_{2}}{|b_{\perp}|^{7}}\epsilon(u_{1}a_{1}b_{\perp}\mu)\,,

(6.71)

\displaystyle\langle\Delta\mathbb{S}_{1}^{\mu}\rangle_{\tilde{G}_{3}}

\displaystyle=-\left(\frac{\kappa}{2}\right)^{6}\frac{135}{128\pi}\frac{1}{\sqrt{\sigma^{2}-1}}\frac{m_{1}^{2}m_{2}}{|b_{\perp}|^{7}}\epsilon(u_{1}a_{1}b_{\perp}\mu)\,.

(6.72)

Note that the expressions of the impulse (
6.65
)-(
6.68
) only depend on the spin via the projections
a_{i\perp}
defined in (
6.50
), satisfying
u_{i}{\cdot}a_{j\perp}{=}0
. This also happens for the spin kick (
6.70
) for the
G_{3}
deformation at linear order in the spin, however at
\mathcal{O}(a_{i}^{2})
and beyond this is no longer true. The spin kicks associated to
I_{1}
,
\tilde{I}_{1}
and
\tilde{G_{3}}
in (
6.69
), (
6.71
) and (
6.72
) generically depend on all the components of the four-vectors
a_{i}^{\mu}
.

6.4.3
Aligned spin case

An interesting situation to discuss is the aligned-spin case, where

\displaystyle p_{i}{\cdot}a_{j}=0\,,\qquad{a}_{i}{\cdot}{b_{\perp}}=0\,,\qquad i,j=1,2\,.

(6.73)

For instance, as in
[
31
]

one could pick an orthonormal basis

\{e^{\mu}_{\hat{a}}\}_{a=0}^{3}
, defined such that
\eta_{\mu\nu}e^{\mu}_{\hat{a}}e^{\nu}_{\hat{b}}{=}\delta_{ab}
and
\epsilon_{\mu\nu\alpha\beta}e^{\mu}_{\hat{0}}e^{\nu}_{\hat{1}}e^{\alpha}_{\hat{2}}e^{\beta}_{\hat{3}}=1
, and align the spins as
a^{\mu}_{1,2}{=}a_{1,2}\,e^{\mu}_{\hat{3}}
, the impact parameter as
b^{\mu}{=}b\,e^{\mu}_{\hat{1}}
,
with the velocities
u_{1}
and
u_{2}
being in the
(\hat{0},\hat{2})
plane.
In this configuration the spin vectors of the two bodies are then aligned with the orbital (and hence also total) angular momentum of the system. Note that in the aligned case the four-vectors
a_{i}
and
a_{i\perp}
actually coincide.

The aligned-spin expressions for
\langle\Delta\mathbb{P}_{1}^{\mu}\rangle_{I}
and

\langle\Delta\mathbb{S}_{1}^{\mu}\rangle_{I}
, for
I\in(I_{1},G_{3},\tilde{I}_{1},\tilde{G}_{3})
, are found to be schematically of the form:

\displaystyle\langle\Delta\mathbb{P}_{1}^{\mu}\rangle_{I_{1},G_{3}}\propto b^{\mu}_{\perp}\,,\qquad\langle\Delta\mathbb{P}_{1}^{\mu}\rangle_{\tilde{I}_{1},\tilde{G}_{3}}\propto a^{\mu}\,,

(6.74)

where
a^{\mu}
is the common direction of
a_{1,2}^{\mu}
, and

\displaystyle\langle\Delta\mathbb{S}_{1}^{\mu}\rangle_{I_{1},G_{3}}=0\,,\qquad\ \langle\Delta\mathbb{S}_{1}^{\mu}\rangle_{\tilde{I}_{1},\tilde{G}_{3}}=C_{\tilde{I}_{1},\tilde{G}_{3}}u_{1}^{\mu}+D_{\tilde{I}_{1},\tilde{G}_{3}}\epsilon(u_{1}a_{1}b\mu)\,,

(6.75)

where
C_{i},\,D_{i}
are complicated kinematic-dependent factors.

A few comments are in order here. First, we note that for parity-even interactions the motion lies on the plane defined by the relative momentum
\vec{p}
and the impact parameter
\vec{b}_{\perp}
, and the spin is conserved.
On the other hand, for parity-odd interactions the situation is substantially different: the impulse of particle
1
lies in the direction of the spins, hence the motion is nonplanar, see also

[
158
,
159
,
160
]
for related observations.

If the orbits lie on a plane, one can also define a scattering angle
\chi
, given by
(see e.g.
[
34
,
9
]
)

\displaystyle\sin\frac{\chi}{2}=-\frac{1}{2{|\vec{p}\,|}}\frac{\partial}{\partial b}\delta_{\text{as}}\,,

(6.76)

where

\displaystyle|\vec{p}\,|=\frac{m_{1}m_{2}\sqrt{\sigma^{2}-1}}{E}=\frac{m_{1}m_{2}\sqrt{\sigma^{2}-1}}{\sqrt{m_{1}^{2}+m_{2}^{2}+2m_{1}m_{2}\sigma}}\,

(6.77)

is the (common) momentum in the centre of mass frame, and
\delta_{\text{as}}\coloneqq\delta\big|_{p_{i}\cdot a_{j}=a_{i}\cdot b_{\perp}=0}
with
\delta(b)=-i\mathcal{M}_{4}(b)
, where
\mathcal{M}_{4}(b)
is the one-loop elastic amplitude in impact-parameter space. Furthermore,
since we are working to leading order in the perturbations, we can approximate
\sin(\chi/2)\approx\chi/2
. We denote
\chi_{\mathcal{F}_{1}}
and
\chi_{\mathcal{F}_{2}}
the contributions to the scattering angle from the Fourier transforms
\mathcal{F}_{1}
and
\mathcal{F}_{2}
in (
6.33
) respectively, and obtain

\displaystyle\chi_{\mathcal{F}_{1}}

\displaystyle=\frac{45|b|E}{4\pi\sqrt{\sigma^{2}-1}m_{1}m_{2}}\Bigg\{\frac{1}{(b^{2}-a_{+}^{2})^{7/2}}+\frac{1}{(b^{2}-a_{-}^{2})^{7/2}}\Bigg\}

(6.78)

\displaystyle\chi_{\mathcal{F}_{2}}

\displaystyle=i\frac{15E}{4\pi m_{1}m_{2}}\Bigg\{\frac{a_{+}(18b^{6}-35a_{+}^{2}b^{4}+28a_{+}^{4}b^{2}-8a_{+}^{6})}{b^{6}(b^{2}-a_{+}^{2})^{7/2}}

\displaystyle-\frac{a_{-}(18b^{6}-35a_{-}^{2}b^{4}+28a_{-}^{4}b^{2}-8a_{-}^{6})}{b^{6}(b^{2}-a_{-}^{2})^{7/2}}\Bigg\}\,.

(6.79)

where
a_{+}=a_{1}+a_{2}
and
a_{-}=a_{1}-a_{2}
. The deflection angle for the
I_{1}
and
G_{3}
interactions can be written in terms of these building blocks:

\displaystyle\chi_{I_{1}}=

\displaystyle\left(\frac{\kappa}{2}\right)^{6}\frac{3}{64}\frac{m_{1}^{2}m_{2}}{\sqrt{\sigma^{2}-1}}\left[(\sigma^{2}-1)\chi_{\mathcal{F}_{1}}-i\sigma\chi_{\mathcal{F}_{2}}\right]+(1\leftrightarrow 2)\,,

(6.80)

\displaystyle\chi_{G_{3}}=

\displaystyle\left(\frac{\kappa}{2}\right)^{6}\frac{3}{64}\frac{m_{1}^{2}m_{2}}{\sqrt{\sigma^{2}-1}}(-\chi_{\mathcal{F}_{1}})+(1\leftrightarrow 2)\,,

(6.81)

where by
(1\leftrightarrow 2)
we mean swapping
m_{1}{\leftrightarrow}m_{2}
,
a_{+}{\rightarrow}a_{+}
and
a_{-}{\rightarrow}-a_{-}
.
For the parity-odd case, applying (
6.76
) yields

\displaystyle\chi_{\tilde{\mathcal{F}}_{1}}=

\displaystyle\chi_{\tilde{\mathcal{F}}_{2}}=0\,.

(6.82)

This reflects the fact that the particles are deflected in the perpendicular direction to the scattering plane, as we can see from (
6.74
).

7
Conclusions

In this paper we have studied the effect of all independent parity-even and parity-odd cubic deformations on the dynamics of two Kerr black holes.
When looking at two-to-two scattering, the leading-order corrections first appear at one loop. By including the effects of spin, we obtained the relevant Compton amplitudes, an essential ingredient for the calculation – which we performed both in the leading singularity and HEFT approaches. With the elastic amplitude in hand, we computed a number of classical observables using the KMOC formalism: the impulse, the spin kick, and the scattering angle for all cubic deformations; their full expressions can be found in the
Cubic Corrections to Spinning Observables from Amplitudes
GitHub repository
.
Examining these in the aligned spin limit, we observe planar motion for the parity-even interactions, as in the Einstein-Hilbert case. On the other hand, parity-odd interactions lead to non-planar scattering, kicking the black holes off the
scattering plane.

There are several interesting directions to examine further. A natural next step is to look at how these cubic deformations affect the waveform when spin is included. In particular, the parity-odd terms are of interest due to their peculiar effects on the dynamics of the spinning objects. As mentioned in the introduction, in the scalar case the parity-even interaction
G_{3}
can can be reinterpreted as a tidal
deformation after a field redefinition, as hinted at by the lack of a pole in
(
2.23
)
. It would be interesting to see if this statement can be extended to the spinning case for both
G_{3}
and its parity-odd version
\tilde{G}_{3}
, with the latter not contributing to the four-point scalar amplitude at one loop. Another avenue is looking at how other higher-derivative corrections, such as
R^{4}
interactions, behave in the presence of spin.
We will come back to these questions in the near future.
